    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-bracket-identity",
        "exact_quote": "Fix a window system $\\mathcal W$ for a finite grid $\\mathcal L$, and either bracket from <a href=\"#lem:boundary-brackets\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:boundary-brackets\">33</a>. Let $K_0,\\ldots,K_q\\Subset D$ be disks, with $K_0$ at positive distance from every $K_j$, $j\\ge1$, and let $X_\\delta,F_\\delta$ be as in <a href=\"#eq:pins-insertion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pins-insertion\">[eq:pins-insertion]</a>. Suppose there are two levels $\\ell_-<\\ell_+$ in $\\mathcal L$ such that:*\n\n1.  *$K_0$ is strictly between the two levels, and both levels avoid every spectator disk $K_j$ with positive margin;*\n\n2.  *after opening sufficiently small gaps inside their corresponding windows, every pin arc and spectator disk in the middle band belongs to one of the two strictly separated $u$-projection families in <a href=\"#eq:middle-projections\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:middle-projections\">[eq:middle-projections]</a>.*\n\n\n*Then $$\\label{eq:bracket-identity}\n \\lim_{\\delta\\downarrow0}\n   \\mathbb E_{\\mu^{\\pm,\\mathcal W}_\\delta}\n       [H_\\delta(\\Delta\\phi)F_\\delta]=0.$$ In particular it applies to products of bounded continuous functions of finitely many smooth zero-total tests in each $K_j$. There is no disjointness requirement between spectator disks. For this latter class the identity passes to any subsequential distributional limit of the bracket laws.*\n\n*For any fixed bound $q$ on the number of spectator disks these geometric hypotheses can be arranged locally as follows. Fix $x\\in D$ and $\\rho>0$ with $\\overline{B(x,\\rho)}\\subset D$. Take $K_0$ sufficiently small about $x$, and take at most $q$ spectator disks of sufficiently small radii, all outside $B(x,\\rho)$. From a sufficiently dense grid of regular levels one can choose the two levels within a fixed small fraction of $\\rho$ above and below $x$. The choices and smallness bounds depend on $q$ and $\\rho$, not on the number of terms in a later linear combination of such products.",
        "source_id": "PM-232.2"
      },

        {

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-boundary-arches",
        "exact_quote": "There are constants $M>1$, $c,C>0$, and a positive geometric tolerance with the following property. Fix a positive integer $N$ and scales $$\\label{eq:arch-scales}\n  16r<\\ell_1<\\cdots<\\ell_N<R/10,\n  \\qquad \\ell_{i+1}\\ge M\\ell_i.$$ Suppose that, in $Q_R$, the limiting boundary is the single graph $y=f(x)$, with $f(0)=f'(0)=0$ and $\\|f'\\|_\\infty$ smaller than the geometric tolerance, and that its inward side is above this graph. The original polygon boundary is a sufficiently fine uniform parametric approximation of this arc and has the same inside and outside away from its shrinking boundary neighborhood. Its approximation error, divided by $\\ell_1$, tends to zero. A smooth boundary has these properties after decreasing the fixed outer scale. Allow, in addition, a fixed finite number of boundary-attached patch replacements as above, whose diameters are sufficiently small relative to the selected scales. Suppose that the actual usable boundary data satisfy $$\\label{eq:arch-boundary-data}\n \\eta\\ge k-2\\quad\\hbox{everywhere},\n \\qquad\n \\eta\\ge k\\quad\\hbox{on the usable boundary in }Q_R\\setminus Q_r,$$ where $k\\in\\mathbb Z$. For sufficiently small mesh and sufficiently fine boundary and replacement approximations, $$\\label{eq:arch-high-probability}\n \\mathbb P_\\eta\\bigl(\\text{an arch at threshold }k\\text{ surrounds }Q_r\n       \\text{ in }Q_R\\bigr)\\ge 1-Ce^{-cN}.$$ The constants do not depend on the boundary heights or on the number of vertices in a boundary or separator. They also do not depend on the fixed number of tiny replacements; the required approximation accuracy may do so. There is a reversed statement with $\\eta\\le k+2$, clean data $\\eta\\le k$, and a separator at height at most $k$.",
        "source_id": "PM-232.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-boundary-arches",
          "exact_quote": "There are constants $M>1$, $c,C>0$, and a positive geometric tolerance with the following property. Fix a positive integer $N$ and scales $$\\label{eq:arch-scales}\n  16r<\\ell_1<\\cdots<\\ell_N<R/10,\n  \\qquad \\ell_{i+1}\\ge M\\ell_i.$$ Suppose that, in $Q_R$, the limiting boundary is the single graph $y=f(x)$, with $f(0)=f'(0)=0$ and $\\|f'\\|_\\infty$ smaller than the geometric tolerance, and that its inward side is above this graph. The original polygon boundary is a sufficiently fine uniform parametric approximation of this arc and has the same inside and outside away from its shrinking boundary neighborhood. Its approximation error, divided by $\\ell_1$, tends to zero. A smooth boundary has these properties after decreasing the fixed outer scale. Allow, in addition, a fixed finite number of boundary-attached patch replacements as above, whose diameters are sufficiently small relative to the selected scales. Suppose that the actual usable boundary data satisfy $$\\label{eq:arch-boundary-data}\n \\eta\\ge k-2\\quad\\hbox{everywhere},\n \\qquad\n \\eta\\ge k\\quad\\hbox{on the usable boundary in }Q_R\\setminus Q_r,$$ where $k\\in\\mathbb Z$. For sufficiently small mesh and sufficiently fine boundary and replacement approximations, $$\\label{eq:arch-high-probability}\n \\mathbb P_\\eta\\bigl(\\text{an arch at threshold }k\\text{ surrounds }Q_r\n       \\text{ in }Q_R\\bigr)\\ge 1-Ce^{-cN}.$$ The constants do not depend on the boundary heights or on the number of vertices in a boundary or separator. They also do not depend on the fixed number of tiny replacements; the required approximation accuracy may do so. There is a reversed statement with $\\eta\\le k+2$, clean data $\\eta\\le k$, and a separator at height at most $k$.",
          "source_id": "PM-232.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There are constants $M>1$, $c,C>0$, and a positive geometric tolerance with the following property. Fix a positive integer $N$ and scales $$\\label{eq:arch-scales}\n  16r<\\ell_1<\\cdots<\\ell_N<R/10,\n  \\qquad \\ell_{i+1}\\ge M\\ell_i.$$ Suppose that, in $Q_R$, the limiting boundary is the single graph $y=f(x)$, with $f(0)=f'(0)=0$ and $\\|f'\\|_\\infty$ smaller than the geometric tolerance, and that its inward side is above this graph. The original polygon boundary is a sufficiently fine uniform parametric approximation of this arc and has the same inside and outside away from its shrinking boundary neighborhood. Its approximation error, divided by $\\ell_1$, tends to zero. A smooth boundary has these properties after decreasing the fixed outer scale. Allow, in addition, a fixed finite number of boundary-attached patch replacements as above, whose diameters are sufficiently small relative to the selected scales. Suppose that the actual usable boundary data satisfy $$\\label{eq:arch-boundary-data}\n \\eta\\ge k-2\\quad\\hbox{everywhere},\n \\qquad\n \\eta\\ge k\\quad\\hbox{on the usable boundary in }Q_R\\setminus Q_r,$$ where $k\\in\\mathbb Z$. For sufficiently small mesh and sufficiently fine boundary and replacement approximations, $$\\label{eq:arch-high-probability}\n \\mathbb P_\\eta\\bigl(\\text{an arch at threshold }k\\text{ surrounds }Q_r\n       \\text{ in }Q_R\\bigr)\\ge 1-Ce^{-cN}.$$ The constants do not depend on the boundary heights or on the number of vertices in a boundary or separator. They also do not depend on the fixed number of tiny replacements; the required approximation accuracy may do so. There is a reversed statement with $\\eta\\le k+2$, clean data $\\eta\\le k$, and a separator at height at most $k$.",
      "explanation": "Near a constant boundary arc with clean lower bounds on the boundary data, an open arch separator at the given threshold surrounds the inner box with probability exponentially close to one in the number of scales.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-extremal-stopping",
        "exact_quote": "In a deterministic annular strip, good surrounding simple site circuits have inner and outer envelopes. The innermost such circuit can be selected by revealing the strip from its inner side; the outermost can be selected from its outer side. In each case the selection and the revealed values are measurable with respect to the sites on the circuit and on the tested side. No height on the untested side is conditioned by the selection.*\n\n*In particular, in each successful block of <a href=\"#lem:preliminary-circuits\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:preliminary-circuits\">40</a>, select an innermost circuit $C_i^-$ in the inner strip and an outermost circuit $C_i^+$ in the outer strip. Conditional on these selections and the full configuration outside the intervening free regions, those regions have independent Gibbs interiors with their revealed boundary values.",
        "source_id": "PM-232.2"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-boundary-attachment",
        "exact_quote": "Consider a retained middle problem at scale $\\ell$. Near either of the locations $(-2\\ell,0)$ and $(2\\ell,0)$, there are fixed constants $0<d/W_0\\ll1$ and $0<W_0/\\ell\\ll1$ for which the following holds. If the local boundary and the tiny replacements are within a sufficiently small fraction of $d$ of the horizontal boundary line, then with probability at least $a_0>0$ a path of sites at height at least $k$ enters from the clean boundary band and reaches depth $d$ inside the box of half-width $W_0$. Its boundary end is connected through good fixed or artificial sites to the exterior below, within a slight enlargement of the box. The bound is uniform in the exposed boundary values of the middle problem.",

        "anchor": "stmt-cor-boundary-robustness",
        "exact_quote": "The arch estimate remains valid conditional on an exterior sigma-field whenever, conditional on that field, the remaining free heights have a uniform Gibbs law and their actual usable boundary data obey <a href=\"#eq:arch-boundary-data\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:arch-boundary-data\">[eq:arch-boundary-data]</a>.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-boundary-references",
        "exact_quote": "Fix a compact subarc of one open constant boundary arc, with value $k$. There exist finite averages of comparable-depth unit smearings near that subarc which converge to $k$ in every finite $L^p$ of the true limiting field. More precisely, choose $N$ distinct points $p_1,\\ldots,p_N$ in the subarc, let $x_i(t)$ approach $p_i$ normally at depth $t$, and put $$A_{N,t}=\\frac1N\\sum_{i=1}^N\n       H(\\rho_{x_i(t),\\kappa d(x_i(t))}).$$ For all sufficiently small $t$ the balls are disjoint, and $$\\limsup_{t\\downarrow0}\\mathbb E|A_{N,t}-k|^2\\le\\frac C N,\n \\qquad\n \\sup_{N,t}\\norm{A_{N,t}-k}_{L^q}\\le C_q.\n \\label{eq:boundary-average-variance}$$ The second supremum is over admissible small depths. Consequently $\\lim_{N\\to\\infty}\\limsup_{t\\downarrow0}\n\\norm{A_{N,t}-k}_{L^p}=0$ for every finite $p$.",
        "source_id": "PM-232.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-boundary-references",
          "exact_quote": "Fix a compact subarc of one open constant boundary arc, with value $k$. Consequently $\\lim_{N\\to\\infty}\\limsup_{t\\downarrow0}\n\\norm{A_{N,t}-k}_{L^p}=0$ for every finite $p$.",
          "source_id": "PM-232.2"
        }

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **double-B edge, path and circuit** ([source](#stmt-auto-definition-1)): A dual edge is *double-$B$ of sign $t$* if the two primal endpoints of the crossed edge both have $B=t$.
- **coherent pair** ([source](#eq:gray-code)): A pair $(B,W)$ is *coherent* if the two spins never both change across one edge.
- **graph inradius** ([source](#pm-h-12)): The *graph inradius* about $z$ of a simple contour $L$ surrounding $z$ is the largest integer $n$ for which $\Lambda_n(z)$ and $\Gamma_n(z)$ lie strictly inside $L$.
- **occupied plus site** ([source](#pm-h-12)): At edge weight one, a site is *occupied plus* if all three incident faces have $B=+1$.
- **separated obstacles** ([source](#pm-h-15)): A family of *separated obstacles* consists of finitely many supports, each contained in a bounded simply connected polygonal neighborhood $U_i$, with the closures $\overline U_i$ pairwise disjoint.
- **zero-total smearing** ([source](#eq:normalized-weights)): A *zero-total* smearing also has $\sum_xw_x=0$, so it is independent of the choice of height anchor.
- **window** ([source](#pm-h-31)): Around every intersection choose a small open boundary arc, called a *window*.
- **skeleton** ([source](#pm-h-36)): A *skeleton* is a finite connected union of lattice edges.
- **arch separator** ([source](#pm-h-36)): An open site circuit surrounding $Q_r$ is called an *arch separator*: its part in the domain joins the clean boundary on the two sides, and its remaining part can run through the artificial exterior.
- **unit triangular lattice** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Write $$\mathbb T=\mathbb Z(1,0)+\mathbb Z(1/2,\sqrt3/2)$$ for the unit triangular lattice.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-sigma-spectral | odd-height multiplier sigma | = | $4\pi\sqrt{c_*}$ | Universal; depends only on the lattice model. |
| q-variance-multiplier | integer-height variance multiplier v | = | $(2\pi)^2c_*$ | Universal constant. |
| q-stack-failure | probability of no circuit in a logarithmic stack | ≤ | $C(r/L)^\alpha$ | Uniform for the radius ratio large. |
| q-plane-tv-decay | total variation distance to the plane law | ≤ | $C(r/L)^\alpha$ | Restriction to a concentric ball of radius r. |
| q-pin-product-upper | joint probability of separated pin events | ≤ | $C\prod_i p_i$ | Constants depend on collars, number of obstacles and path counts, not on mesh or pin values. |
| q-smearing-moment | moment of an interior smearing | ≤ | $C_p\bigl(1+\sqrt{\log(n/a)}\bigr)$ | Radius a at distance n from the boundary. |
| q-plane-zero-total-moment | moment of a zero-total smearing in the plane | ≤ | $C_p$ | Uniform in radius, location and rotation. |
| q-switch-variance | conditional variance of a zero-total statistic under the component switch | ≥ | $4\lambda^2$ | Total weight lambda inside the inner cut. |
| q-annulus-spectral-mass | spectral mass of a dyadic annulus | ≤ | $K$ | Small radii. |
| q-vertical-period | period of the reciprocal vertical circle | = | $2\pi/\sqrt3$ | Fixed lattice constant. |
| q-dispersion-defect | dispersion defect D(k,s) | = | $\left(\frac{\|k\|-s}{\|k\|+s}\right)^2$ | Up to an error linear in the horizontal frequency. |
| q-endcap-mass | spectral mass of an endcap | ≤ | $K_3\frac{\varepsilon}{r}$ | Thin strips of width epsilon at radius r. |
| q-cell-area | area of the triangular-lattice cell | = | $\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$ | Fixed. |
| q-normal-step | normal translation step between site rows | = | $\sqrt3\,\delta$ | Mesh delta positive. |
| q-arch-probability | probability of an arch separator | ≥ | $1-Ce^{-cN}$ | Constants independent of boundary heights. |
| q-collision-coefficient | logarithmic collision coefficient | = | $\frac{v}{2\pi}T_{j-2}(\mathbf z)$ | Uncentered, with the convention for no points. |
| q-odd-sigma-relation | odd-height multiplier in terms of v | = | $2\sqrt v$ | Universal. |
| q-circuit-slack | slack parameter of local circuits | < | $1/50$ | Fixed small parameter. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-gray** [claim](#stmt-lem-gray) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gray): Inspect consecutive Gray states, sum around triangles, and reconstruct heights from loop orientations. -> Curl-free lift and loop representation
- **s-fkg** [claim](#stmt-lem-spin-fkg) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spin-fkg): Encode second-spin walls over the two-element field, use supermodularity of nullity to get the lattice condition. -> First-spin FKG with equality constraints
- **s-double-cut** [claim](#stmt-lem-double-cut) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-double-cut): Factor the counting weight across a double circuit and use a stopping exploration revealing only exterior first spins. -> Exact cut law
- **s-local-circuit** [claim](#stmt-lem-local-circuit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-local-circuit): Lower with minus boundary by FKG, apply the cited annulus input and lift occupied circuits to double circuits. -> Local double circuits
- **s-worst-cut** [claim](#stmt-lem-worst-cut) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-worst-cut): Cover a polygonal curve by small annuli, glue intersecting circuits and multiply by FKG. -> Circuit under opposite cut
- **s-buffer** [claim](#stmt-lem-buffer-circuits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-buffer-circuits): Glue small annulus circuits along collars and tubes; iterate over disjoint slots to get the power-law stack bound. -> Circuits in buffers
- **s-loop-est** [claim](#stmt-prop-loop-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-loop-estimates): Use opposite-sign collars for the lower bound; for the upper bound select a stopped plus cut and test small annuli near an exterior face. -> Loop estimates
- **s-plane-mixing** [claim](#stmt-prop-plane-mixing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-plane-mixing): Common-lower-law coupling with a stack of plus circuits, then Cauchy property of cylinder laws. -> Plane law and local comparison
- **s-rel-pin** [claim](#stmt-lem-relative-pin-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-relative-pin-comparison): Sandwich the pin probability between a deterministic-cut value and a closer stopped-cut value. -> Relative comparison
- **s-pin-product** [claim](#stmt-prop-pin-product) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-pin-product): Multiply successive relative comparisons for equality constraints, plus pins, minus pins and restored labels. -> Product bounds for pin events
- **s-midpoint** [claim](#stmt-lem-midpoint-tails) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-midpoint-tails): Apply the rounded midpoint inequality to indicators of dyadic intervals and iterate the tail recursion. -> Exponential tails and moments
- **s-loop-shells** [claim](#stmt-lem-loop-shells) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-loop-shells): Expose surrounding loops in ratio intervals and dominate counts by geometric variables via the loop upper estimate. -> Loop count moments
- **s-smearing** [claim](#stmt-prop-smearing-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-smearing-moments): Bound the common-loop covariance by arm-blocking circuits, then amplify a Chebyshev central bound by midpoint tails. -> Interior smearing moments
- **s-plane-moments** [claim](#stmt-prop-plane-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-plane-moments): Search an exterior double circuit, bound the shifted interior by the smearing estimate, then amplify and pass to the plane limit. -> Plane zero-total moments
- **s-switch** [claim](#stmt-lem-component-switch) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-component-switch): Use the component potential identity to locate a collar component whose fair sign flip shifts inner heights. -> Fair switch and variance lower bound
- **s-obstacle-moments** [claim](#stmt-prop-obstacle-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-obstacle-moments): Anchor at one obstacle, split by offset parities, use switches to equalize class mass and amplify by midpoint tails. -> Conditional obstacle moments
- **s-equal-offset** [claim](#stmt-cor-equal-offset-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-equal-offset-moments): With equal offsets use one class and the central probability from the exterior cut search. -> Uniform moments with varying separations
- **s-reflection-pos** [claim](#stmt-prop-reflection-positivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-reflection-positivity): Row conditioning gives positivity; composing two reflections gives a normal translation whose log-convex norms make it a contraction. -> Positive normal transfer
- **s-incr-spectral** [claim](#stmt-lem-increment-spectral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-increment-spectral): Spectral resolution of translations plus the cocycle identity; the invariant part vanishes by moment bounds. -> Increment spectral measure
- **s-annulus-mass** [claim](#stmt-lem-annulus-mass) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-annulus-mass): Test with two smooth zero-integral functions whose transforms have no common zero on the annulus. -> Uniform annular mass
- **s-cauchy** [claim](#stmt-prop-cauchy-mixture) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-cauchy-mixture): Joint spectral resolution of normal transfer and tangential translation gives a Cauchy mixture for the projected measure. -> Positive Cauchy mixture
- **s-angular-poisson** [claim](#stmt-lem-angular-poisson) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-angular-poisson): Poisson formula for a bounded analytic function on the half-plane, then control periodization. -> Angular Poisson identity
- **s-dispersion** [claim](#stmt-prop-dispersion-defect) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-dispersion-defect): Integrate the Poisson identity, use that the fourth harmonic averages to zero under rotations, and bound endcaps by annuli. -> Summable defect and band bound
- **s-endcap** [claim](#stmt-lem-endcap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-endcap): Rotate by a lattice angle and bound Cauchy densities on a thin interval, using the band mass bound. -> Endcap estimate
- **s-spectral-sub** [claim](#stmt-lem-spectral-subsequences) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spectral-subsequences): Compactness of rescaled measures; the defect forces support on the dispersion graph; rotations force a constant density. -> Form of subsequential limits
- **s-angular-sum** [claim](#stmt-prop-angular-sum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-angular-sum): Telescope dyadic cutoffs, subtract the vanishing radial integral and evaluate the polar integral of the fourth harmonic against a logarithm. -> Angular sum rule fixing the coefficient
- **s-plane-cov** [claim](#stmt-thm-plane-covariance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-plane-covariance): Pass from spectral convergence to test covariance by transform bounds; positivity by the component switch. -> Universal plane covariance
- **s-refl-null** [claim](#stmt-cor-reflection-null) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-reflection-null): The squared reflection norm converges to a pairing of disjoint supports, which is zero. -> Reflection null vector
- **s-normalization** [claim](#stmt-prop-normalization-series) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-normalization-series): Relate band coefficients to the spectral integrals, bound finite-volume errors by coupling, and sum the telescoping series. -> Finite-volume normalization series
- **s-brackets** [claim](#stmt-lem-boundary-brackets) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-boundary-brackets): Propagate one pair of integers along constant-spin chains and use height comparison for the stochastic order. -> Bracket laws and coupling
- **s-norm-reflection** [claim](#stmt-lem-normalized-reflection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-normalized-reflection): Expand the reflected square, apply the pin product bound to the doubled family and conditional moments to the insertion. -> Normalized reflection bound
- **s-analytic** [claim](#stmt-lem-analytic-translation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-analytic-translation): Define holomorphic matrix elements of transfer powers, bounded uniformly, and apply a normal-family and identity-theorem argument. -> Analytic translation principle
- **s-gapped** [claim](#stmt-prop-gapped-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-gapped-identity): Move obstacle families apart in three lattice normal directions, use the reflection null vector, then remove moves by analytic continuation; Fourier-project equal offsets. -> Gapped identity
- **s-gap-filling** [claim](#stmt-lem-gap-filling) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gap-filling): Circuits around each gap force equal offsets and couple gapped and filled laws; equal-offset moments give uniform bounds. -> Gap filling
- **s-bracket-identity** [claim](#stmt-prop-bracket-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-bracket-identity): Divide the equal-offset identity by its probability, truncate the insertion and send the gap to zero before truncation. -> Bracket identity
- **s-prelim-circuits** [claim](#stmt-lem-preliminary-circuits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-preliminary-circuits): Synthetic two-interval comparison, buffer circuits per scale and an exponential moment bound on the success count. -> Many preliminary pairs
- **s-extremal** [claim](#stmt-lem-extremal-stopping) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-extremal-stopping): Envelope operation on surrounding cycles selects extremal circuits from one side, leaving Gibbs interiors. -> Stopping sigma-field
- **s-attachment** [claim](#stmt-lem-boundary-attachment) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-boundary-attachment): Synthetic mean at a probe site detects the high side of open contours; low lateral barriers force a high path to rise. -> Boundary attachment
- **s-arch-upgrade** [claim](#stmt-lem-arch-upgrade) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-arch-upgrade): Join two attachments by crossbars and bulk tubes using positive association of increasing circuit events. -> Upgraded arch in a middle region
- **s-boundary-arches** [claim](#stmt-thm-boundary-arches) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-arch-upgrade-2): Count successful blocks, then use conditional independence of retained regions to get exponential failure. -> Constant-arc arch estimate
- **s-robust** [claim](#stmt-cor-boundary-robustness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-boundary-robustness): All lower bounds are uniform in the trace, so they apply under conditioning and for tiny replacements. -> Robust arch estimate
- **s-tightness** [claim](#stmt-prop-tightness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-tightness): Smearing moments on a partition of unity and Fourier-basis bounds give tightness in a negative Sobolev space. -> Tightness and moment convergence
- **s-boundary-trace** [claim](#stmt-prop-boundary-trace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-boundary-trace): Censor arches and a final arch give one-sided mean bounds near a constant arc; harmonic uniqueness and ordering collapse the brackets. -> Boundary trace and mixed moments
- **s-boundary-refs** [claim](#stmt-lem-boundary-references) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-boundary-references): Expand the square of the average and use the mixed boundary trace for cross terms. -> Boundary reference averages
- **s-harmonicity** [claim](#stmt-prop-moment-harmonicity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-moment-harmonicity): Transport each absolute observation to boundary references through local zero-total pieces and apply the bracket identity. -> Separate harmonicity
- **s-no-contact** [claim](#stmt-lem-no-contact) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-no-contact): Common mollification with polylogarithmic bounds shows integrability and no diagonal term; boundary trace from the mixed estimate. -> No diagonal distributions
- **s-log-sing** [claim](#stmt-lem-log-singularity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-log-singularity): Fourier expansion of a harmonic function on a punctured disk with growth bounds kills negative modes. -> Logarithmic singularity
- **s-collision** [claim](#stmt-prop-collision-coefficient) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-collision-coefficient): Stopped coupling with the plane law identifies the local product, which is compared with the logarithmic expansion. -> Collision coefficient
- **s-gaussian** [claim](#stmt-thm-gaussian-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-gaussian-moments): The difference of both sides of the recursion is bounded harmonic with zero trace; then determinacy of Gaussian moments. -> Gaussian moment recursion
- **s-field-limit** [claim](#stmt-cor-field-limit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-field-limit): Tightness plus unique identification of limits gives full convergence; centering and affine relation give the odd normalization. -> Full field convergence
- **s-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-field-limit): Combine full convergence with the finite-volume normalization of the constant. -> Main theorem

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-main-scaling-limit**: For the uniform odd integer height with two-arc boundary data on triangular-lattice approximations, the field converges in distributions to the harmonic extension of the boundary data plus a universal positive multiple of the Dirichlet Gaussian free field; test-function means converge to the integral of the harmonic extension.
- **inv-plane-covariance**: The rescaled increment spectral measures converge vaguely on the punctured plane to a positive finite multiple of the inverse squared norm density, which fixes the plane covariance of zero-integral smeared heights.
- **inv-normalization-series**: The normalization constant is given by an absolutely convergent series of finite-volume quantities, and the field multiplier is the stated multiple of the square root of the spectral coefficient.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-real-valued-model**: Apply the Gaussian free field limit and its multiplier to continuous real-valued Lipschitz surfaces. -> The proof uses the integer-valued hard-constraint structure (two-spin encoding, loop representation); the real-valued model is a different problem.
- **anti-pointwise-heights**: Read the limit as a statement about pointwise height values or rescale the field by a logarithm. -> The limit exists only for smooth spatial averages; point fluctuations diverge logarithmically.
- **anti-interface-claim**: Infer convergence of the zero interface from the field limit. -> The result concerns the field only.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main results are asymptotic limit and moment-identification theorems proved by compactness, reflection positivity and harmonic uniqueness; they are not finite procedures. The normalization series is a convergent infinite sum whose finite-volume terms use astronomically large hexagon radii, so no feasible finite computation reproduces a stated bound. The statement of the main theorem begins: There is a constant $\sigma>0$, depending only on the unit triangular-lattice model, such that the field converges in distributions.

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-domain-two-arc** (hypothesis): The main limit is proved for a bounded smooth simply connected planar domain with two distinct marked boundary points, with triangular-lattice approximations whose marked boundary parametrizations converge uniformly. Other topologies or boundary data are not covered. Blocked use: ['Applying the multiplier to domains with holes', 'Applying it to boundaries with more than two arcs or other boundary values']
- **lim-lattice-integer** (hypothesis): The constant sigma depends only on the unit triangular-lattice model with odd integer heights whose neighboring differences are zero or two. Real-valued Lipschitz surfaces and other lattices are outside the statement. Blocked use: ['Using the constant on square-lattice or unstructured meshes', 'Using it for continuous real-valued Lipschitz heights']
- **lim-distributional-only** (formal_scope): Convergence holds in the space of distributions after smooth spatial averaging. Point fluctuations diverge logarithmically, so no pointwise Gaussian statement follows. Blocked use: ['Treating single-site heights as converging to a Gaussian variable', 'Rescaling the field by a logarithm']
- **lim-interface-excluded** (formal_scope): Only the field part of Schramm's problem is resolved. The interface assertion is a separate question and is not implied. Blocked use: ['Claiming convergence of the zero interface to a Schramm-Loewner curve']
- **lim-annulus-input** (hypothesis): The circuit estimates rest on an annulus circuit estimate imported from a companion article, used only at edge weight one. The cut and loop stages inherit this external input. Blocked use: ['Reusing the circuit lemmas at other edge weights without the companion input']
- **lim-order-of-limits** (edge_case): Window, gap and grid limits are taken only after the mesh limit. A lower bound depending on a fixed aspect ratio must not be used as a uniform bound for a degenerating shape. Blocked use: ['Exchanging the mesh limit with gap shrinking or window shrinking', 'Using fixed-geometry constants as uniform bounds when obstacle separations vanish']
- **lim-obstacle-separation** (hypothesis): The conditional obstacle moment bounds assume separated interval-compatible obstacles and a pin-free test neighborhood; no uniformity as separations vanish is asserted. Blocked use: ['Applying the moment bounds with obstacles merging into the test disk or each other']
- **lim-arch-quantifiers** (approximation): The arch estimate is for fixed scales with mesh and boundary error tending to zero afterwards; there is no assertion uniform over unresolved geometry at the mesh scale. Blocked use: ['Using the arch probability bound at scales comparable to the mesh']
- **lim-reflection-norm** (edge_case): The reflection null statement concerns the reflection norm only; the ordinary variance of the Laplace insertion need not vanish. Blocked use: ['Concluding that the Laplace-smeared height has zero variance']
- **lim-normalization-infinite** (nonconstructive): The normalization is an absolutely convergent infinite series whose finite-volume terms use very large radii, so it is a specification and not a practical computation. Blocked use: ['Presenting the series as a feasible numerical routine']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the centered uniform odd integer height function on triangular-lattice approximations of a smooth simply connected domain, with neighboring differences zero or two and boundary values +1 and −1 on two arcs, converges to a universal multiple of the Dirichlet Gaussian free field. This resolves the field part of Schramm's Problem 2.2. We give an absolutely convergent finite-volume formula for the normalization. The proof combines reflection positivity, a spectral sum rule, and boundary comparison with Gaussian moment identities.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Uniform Lipschitz heights are among the simplest random surface models with a hard local constraint. Their planar fluctuations are expected to be governed by the Gaussian free field. We prove this for integer heights on the triangular lattice with the two-arc boundary condition proposed by Schramm. The conclusion concerns the field, including all joint test-function limits and distributional tightness.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The model and the limit

Write $$\mathbb T=\mathbb Z(1,0)+\mathbb Z(1/2,\sqrt3/2)$$ for the unit triangular lattice. Let $D\subset\mathbb R^2$ be bounded, smooth, and simply connected, and let $a,b$ be distinct points of $\partial D$. They divide the boundary into open arcs $A_+$ and $A_-$. For $\delta\downarrow0$, let $D_\delta$ be simply connected polygonal domains made of triangles of $\delta\mathbb T$, whose marked boundary parametrizations converge uniformly to that of $(D,a,b)$. Put the discrete marks at the midpoints of distinct boundary edges. The boundary vertices then split into disjoint sets $A_{+,\delta}$ and $A_{-,\delta}$; no vertex receives two prescriptions.

<a id="eq:odd-model"></a>
Choose $h_\delta$ uniformly among the odd integer functions on the vertices of $D_\delta$ satisfying $$\label{eq:odd-model}
 h_\delta=1\text{ on }A_{+,\delta},\qquad
 h_\delta=-1\text{ on }A_{-,\delta},\qquad
 |h_\delta(x)-h_\delta(y)|\in\{0,2\}\quad(x\sim y).$$ Extend the function affinely on every triangle. For a test function $f\in C_c^\infty(D)$, write $h_\delta(f)=\int h_\delta(x)f(x)\,dx$ once its support lies in $D_\delta$. This convention is used for every smeared height below.

<a id="eq:gff-convention"></a>
Our Dirichlet Gaussian free field $\Phi_D$ has covariance $$\label{eq:gff-convention}
 \mathbb E[\Phi_D(f)\Phi_D(g)]
 =\iint_{D\times D}f(x)G_D(x,y)g(y)\,dx\,dy,
 \qquad G_D=(-\Delta_D)^{-1}.$$ In particular, the logarithmic singularity of $G_D(x,y)$ is $(2\pi)^{-1}\log(1/|x-y|)$. Let $\bar g$ be the bounded harmonic extension of the boundary data $+1$ on $A_+$ and $-1$ on $A_-$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $D$ be a bounded smooth simply connected planar domain with two distinct marked boundary points. Let $D_\delta$ be simply connected triangular-lattice polygonal approximations with uniformly converging marked boundary parametrizations and edge-midpoint marks, and let $h_\delta$ have the uniform odd-height law <a href="#eq:odd-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-model">[eq:odd-model]</a>. There is a constant $\sigma>0$, depending only on the unit triangular-lattice model, such that $$h_\delta\ \Longrightarrow\ \bar g+\sigma\Phi_D
 \qquad\text{in }\mathcal D'(D).$$ For every $f\in C_c^\infty(D)$, $\mathbb Eh_\delta(f)\to\int_D\bar g f$. Consequently <a id="eq:main-centered"></a>$$\label{eq:main-centered}
 \frac{h_\delta-\mathbb Eh_\delta}{\sigma}
 \ \Longrightarrow\ \Phi_D
 \qquad\text{in }\mathcal D'(D).$$ All finite vectors of test-function integrals converge jointly to the corresponding Gaussian vectors, and all their mixed moments converge. The constant is specified by the absolutely convergent finite-volume series in <a href="#prop:normalization-series" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:normalization-series">31</a>; in the spectral convention below, $\sigma=4\pi\sqrt{c_*}$.*

</div>

It is convenient to prove the theorem for <a id="eq:integer-height"></a>$$\label{eq:integer-height}
 H_\delta=(h_\delta-1)/2,
 \qquad g=0\text{ on }A_+,\quad g=-1\text{ on }A_-.$$ Thus $H_\delta$ is integer valued and $|H_\delta(x)-H_\delta(y)|\le1$ on edges. Write $m$ for the bounded harmonic extension of $g$, so $\bar g=1+2m$. We will show that $H_\delta$ converges to $m+\sqrt v\,\Phi_D$, where <a id="eq:normalization-relations"></a>$$\label{eq:normalization-relations}
 v=(2\pi)^2c_*,\qquad \sigma^2=4v.$$ There is no logarithmic rescaling of the distribution-valued field. The logarithmic divergence of point fluctuations is consistent with a finite limit after smooth spatial averaging.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### History and significance

Schramm’s 2006 problem collection asks about the scaling limits of this height function and its zero interface (Schramm 2007, Problem 2.2, p. 527) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Schramm2007](#ref-Schramm2007)]. resolves the field part of that problem for smooth simply connected domains, with the covariance convention made explicit in <a href="#eq:gff-convention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gff-convention">[eq:gff-convention]</a>. The interface assertion is a separate question. The real-valued Lipschitz model in Schramm’s Problem 2.3, p. 528, is also distinct from the integer model studied here.

Glazman and Manolescu developed a two-spin representation with positive association for the uniform integer Lipschitz model and studied its logarithmic fluctuations (Glazman and Manolescu 2021) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[GM2021](#ref-GM2021)]. Glazman and Lammers subsequently studied delocalisation across a larger parameter range. Their 2025 article discusses the Gaussian free field limit as a conjecture (Glazman and Lammers 2025, sec. 1.2.4) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[GL2025](#ref-GL2025)]. The scale estimates used here are proved in <a href="#sec:cuts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cuts">3</a> from the annulus circuit estimate specified below. Logarithmic fluctuations alone do not determine a Gaussian scaling limit. The additional work here is to obtain comparisons under rare pinning, identify the covariance, control the imposed boundary values, and determine all joint moments.

For real-valued gradient fields with smooth uniformly convex interactions, Miller proved a bounded-domain GFF limit with continuous perturbations of affine boundary data (Miller 2011, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Miller2011](#ref-Miller2011)]. The integer-valued law studied here, with its hard constraint on every edge increment, lies outside those hypotheses.

Besides the loop estimates, we use the discrete midpoint Prékopa–Leindler inequality of Klartag–Lehec (Klartag and Lehec 2019, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KlartagLehec2019](#ref-KlartagLehec2019)], in the nonnegative-function form of Gozlan–Roberto–Samson–Tetali (Gozlan et al. 2021, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[GRST2019](#ref-GRST2019)]. Its role is to turn central probability estimates into uniform higher moments. The rounding and parity conditions needed for this application are verified in <a href="#sec:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:moments">4</a>.

Reflection positivity is a classical method for converting spatial symmetry and positivity into spectral information (Fröhlich et al. 1978) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[FILS1978](#ref-FILS1978)]. The reflection quotient and positive transfer used here are closely related to the constructions in (Usui 2012, secs. 3.1–3.3 and 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Usui2012](#ref-Usui2012)]. We establish their exact site-row and auxiliary-phase versions directly. The final moment argument has a related predecessor in Kenyon’s identification of dimer height moments by matching collision poles in a determinant pairing identity (Kenyon 2001, Lemma 3.1 and Proposition 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Kenyon2001](#ref-Kenyon2001)]; here the collision coefficients come from local cut mixing rather than a determinant formula.

Duminil-Copin–Kozlowski–Lammers–Manolescu prove a full-plane GFF limit for the zero-slope square-lattice six-vertex state with $a=b=1$ and $c\in[\sqrt3,2]$ (Duminil-Copin et al. 2026, Theorems 2.2 and 2.8) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[DCKLM2026](#ref-DCKLM2026)]. Their transfer representations, analytic translation arguments, harmonic correlations and collision analysis are substantial methodological precedents (Duminil-Copin et al. 2026, Theorems 4.12 and 4.15; Sections 7–9) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[DCKLM2026](#ref-DCKLM2026)]. The argument below uses triangular site reflections and a finite-rotation angular sum to determine the plane covariance. Separated rare pins and boundary attachments then connect this information to the actual two-arc bounded-domain law. The normalization is specified by finite counts and Fourier coefficients in <a href="#sec:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:normalization">6</a>.

For comparison, the prescribed iterated balanced-torus limit of the square-lattice six-vertex model with $a=b=1$ gives a balanced zero-slope plane state with a GFF scaling limit for each fixed $0<c\le2$ (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[OpenAIBalancedSixVertex2026](#ref-OpenAIBalancedSixVertex2026)]. This plane-state result does not identify the present two-arc triangular-lattice law or its normalization.

For weighted integer Lipschitz heights with edge weight $x\in[1/\sqrt2,1]$, a companion article proves an annulus circuit estimate with one spin fixed on the boundary and the other free (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[OpenAIWeighted2026](#ref-OpenAIWeighted2026)]. We use this estimate only at $x=1$, with the first spin fixed to minus and an occupied-plus circuit in the interior as the event. [sec:cuts](#sec:cuts) derives the conditional cut and loop estimates needed here from this input. The weighted field-convergence theorem is not used.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy

<a id="eq:strategy-harmonicity"></a>
The main task is to identify every subsequential limit $H$ of the integer height field. A key intermediate conclusion is $$\label{eq:strategy-harmonicity}
 \mathbb E\left[H(\Delta\phi)\prod_{i=1}^qH(f_i)\right]=0,$$ for smooth compactly supported tests $\phi,f_1,\ldots,f_q$ in $D$ with pairwise disjoint supports, including $q=0$. This says that the moment distributions are harmonic in each variable away from collisions. Boundary traces and the singularities at collisions will then determine those moments. The difficulty is to obtain this identity for absolute heights: reflection positivity first gives a plane identity, and moving pins initially permits only observations of local height differences.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### A spectral identity from reflection positivity.

The height gradient has a stationary plane limit. Reflection through rows of lattice sites gives a positive normal transfer operator. Its spectral representation expresses the covariance as a mixture of Cauchy kernels in the normal frequency. The fourth angular harmonic averages to zero under the six lattice rotations, whereas its Cauchy average is nonnegative up to a summable lattice error. Comparing the two gives an integrable nonnegative dispersion defect. This forces every small-frequency limit to have density $c_*/|p|^2$, and a signed angular sum determines $c_*$ uniquely. For a test $\phi$ supported on one side of a reflection line, the covariance formula makes the covariance of the height average against $\Delta\phi$ with its reflected copy tend to zero. This is the reflection-null identity; it does not assert that the ordinary variance vanishes. The signed angular sum also gives the finite-volume normalization formula in <a href="#sec:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:normalization">6</a>. The spectral argument uses positivity and the additional rotation directions rather than an explicit solution of the model.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Moving rare pins and recovering the boundary.

We encode the height modulo four by two spins taking values $\pm1$ at each vertex; boundary pins prescribe these signs. Boundary pins surround a typical interior insertion, so they cannot all initially lie behind one reflection line. We open short gaps inside boundary windows on which only one of the two spins is pinned. A multiplicative comparison for separated pinning events controls the resulting reflection norms even when the pin events are rare. Positive transfer powers permit analytic continuation of translations in several lattice-normal directions, adapting the method of (Duminil-Copin et al. 2026, sec. 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DCKLM2026](#ref-DCKLM2026)]. This moves the pins past the reflection-null insertion while preserving its limiting identity against observations of local height differences. Circuits carrying the prescribed spin sign then close the gaps and force the disconnected pin arcs to use one common height offset.

To recover the original boundary condition, we prove height-path estimates at a constant boundary arc. The key attachment estimate derives an open level-line connection from a synthetic mean comparison, with explicit stopping rules that preserve the Gibbs law in the unexamined region. The estimate survives the small boundary pieces created when windows are removed behind additional arches. It identifies the harmonic mean and supplies deterministic boundary reference averages.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Harmonic moments and Gaussianity.

Averaging near distinct points of one constant boundary arc gives its prescribed value in the limit, with depths sent to zero before the number of points increases. These reference averages fix the additive constant left undetermined by local height differences and yield <a href="#eq:strategy-harmonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strategy-harmonicity">[eq:strategy-harmonicity]</a> for absolute observations. The resulting moment functions have only logarithmic collision singularities. Local cut mixing and the plane covariance determine each singular coefficient. Subtracting the associated Green functions gives the Gaussian moment recursion. Uniform moment bounds supply both tightness and the passage from moment identities to the centered distributional limit.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Conventions and limit order

The symbols $C,c,\alpha$ denote positive constants, whose values may change between occurrences. Dependence on a fixed finite macroscopic arrangement is allowed when stated. A normalized test at radius $r$ is supported in a ball of radius $r$ and has size $O(r^{-2})$; a zero-total test has integral zero. The Euclidean Fourier convention is $\widehat f(p)=\int e^{-ip\cdot x}f(x)\,dx$.

All arguments with prescribed boxes, collars, or tubes first fix their positive widths and send the lattice mesh to zero. Later limits that shrink windows, refine grids, or approach the boundary are taken only after this mesh limit. In particular, a lower bound depending on a fixed aspect ratio is never used as a uniform bound for a degenerating shape. When comparison curves are random, the specified exploration is stopped before inspecting the region whose conditional law is used.

The proof follows this order. [sec:spin](#sec:spin), [sec:cuts](#sec:cuts), [sec:moments](#sec:moments) establish the spin tools, conditional cuts, and moments. [sec:spectral](#sec:spectral), [sec:normalization](#sec:normalization) identify the plane covariance and its coefficient. [sec:pins](#sec:pins), [sec:boundary](#sec:boundary) transfer the reflection identity and prove the boundary estimate. [sec:limits](#sec:limits), [sec:gaussian](#sec:gaussian) identify all subsequential limits and complete the proof of <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:spin"></a>
### Heights, two spins, and exact cuts

Our first objective is to replace the height constraint by a finite spin law whose conditional distributions can be read exactly. The loop description will connect this law to the scale estimates in <a href="#sec:cuts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cuts">3</a>; the one-color cuts will remain available when rare boundary pins are imposed later. We use the coherent-pair and double-path framework of Glazman–Manolescu (Glazman and Manolescu 2021, Proposition 1.4 and Sections 2.1–2.3) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GM2021](#ref-GM2021)], and establish the versions with equality constraints and stopping cuts needed below.

We use lattice units in the discrete arguments. A finite triangular domain is a finite, simply connected triangular cell complex, with its vertex and edge sets understood. A polygonal boundary, or a separating lattice curve, includes the adjacent fixed vertices when a Gibbs interior is taken. In particular, a free vertex has its full incident star up to fixed boundary vertices. This convention avoids an additional interaction across the boundary of a conditional problem.

Write $H=(h-1)/2$. Thus $H$ is integer-valued and $\abs{H(x)-H(y)}\leq1$ on each edge. Associate two spins to a height by <a id="eq:gray-code"></a>$$\label{eq:gray-code}
 B=+1\quad\hbox{on residues }0,1,\qquad
 W=+1\quad\hbox{on residues }-1,0\pmod4.$$ A pair $(B,W)$ is *coherent* if the two spins never both change across one edge. A free spin law means uniform counting of coherent pairs, without boundary conditions.

<a id="stmt-lem-gray"></a>
<div id="lem:gray" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:height-increment"></a>
**Lemma 2** (The lift and the loop representation). *On a simply connected triangular domain, a coherent pair determines integer height increments $$\label{eq:height-increment}
 dH(x,y)=\frac{W_xB_y-B_xW_y}{2}.$$ They have zero curl. Fixing the height at one vertex gives a bijection between coherent pairs whose Gray state there matches that prescribed integer and admissible heights with that value. Uniform counting has the height Gibbs property.*

*With a constant boundary height, the nonzero height steps form disjoint closed honeycomb loops. Conditional on their geometry, the inward jumps are independent fair signs; the unoriented loop weight is $2^{\#\mathrm{loops}}$. The same conclusion holds in a bounded face of a connected skeleton of fixed lattice edges carrying one constant height.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gray"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gray</summary>

*Proof.* The four Gray states, in increasing residue order, are $(+,+),(+,-),(-,-),(-,+)$. Inspection of consecutive states gives <a href="#eq:height-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-increment">[eq:height-increment]</a>. On a triangle, if $B$ is not constant, it changes on two incident edges; coherence forces $W$ to be constant on all three vertices. Interchanging the colors gives the alternative case. The sum of <a href="#eq:height-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-increment">[eq:height-increment]</a> around that triangle is therefore zero. Every closed path is generated by triangle boundaries, proving path independence and the asserted bijection. Given a separating height trace, all compatible interiors have the same weight, which proves the Gibbs assertion.

A triangle has either no nonzero step or two nonzero steps. In the latter case its three heights take two consecutive values, so the two associated dual edges join into one contour separating those same levels. Contours cannot branch. A constant boundary has no contour endpoints, and each loop may independently be assigned an inward jump $+1$ or $-1$. Summing these jumps from the boundary constructs the inverse height configuration. Thus every unoriented loop configuration has exactly $2^{\#\mathrm{loops}}$ compatible orientations. A loop in a bounded complementary face cannot enclose only part of a connected fixed skeleton: a path in the skeleton from that part to the rest would cross the loop. The same construction consequently applies in such a face. ◻

</details>

The spin comparison must tolerate more than constant boundary conditions. In a finite free domain let $E_0$ be any set of edges on which $W$ is required to be constant, and prescribe arbitrary $B$ values on an arbitrary vertex set. Write $\mu^{E_0,\eta}$ for this constrained pair law. An empty set of $B$ prescriptions is allowed. These constraints are always feasible: take $W$ constant and then assign $B$ arbitrarily.

<a id="stmt-lem-spin-fkg"></a>
<div id="lem:spin-fkg" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Spin FKG with equality constraints). *The $B$ marginal of $\mu^{E_0,\eta}$ satisfies the FKG lattice condition. The assertion remains true after any further $B$ pinnings. In particular, fixing additional $B$ spins to $+1$ increases increasing events, and fixing them to $-1$ decreases such events.*

*Conditional on $B$, the spin $W$ consists of independent fair signs on the components of the graph generated by $E_0$ and the $B$-changing edges.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spin-fkg"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spin-fkg</summary>

*Proof.* Encode a $W$ wall by a binary variable on each edge. Such variables are the differences of vertex signs if and only if their sum around every triangle is zero over $\mathbb F_2$. Simple connectivity makes these triangle equations sufficient; each solution has two vertex lifts. Coherence and $E_0$ require the wall variable to vanish except on equal-$B$ edges outside $E_0$.

Let $A$ be the fixed triangle–edge incidence matrix over $\mathbb F_2$. For a set $F$ of allowed columns put $n(F)=\abs F-\operatorname{rank}(A_F)$. This nullity is increasing and supermodular. Indeed, adding one column increases rank by at most one, and submodularity of matrix rank gives $$n(F\cap G)+n(F\cup G)\geq n(F)+n(G).$$ Let $F_+(B)$ and $F_-(B)$ be the allowed edges whose two $B$ endpoints are respectively $+1$ and $-1$. A triangle cannot contain both a $++$ edge and a $--$ edge. Its parity equations thus split into two disjoint systems, and the number of compatible $W$ configurations is <a id="eq:spin-nullity-weight"></a>$$\label{eq:spin-nullity-weight}
 w(B)=2^{1+n(F_+(B))+n(F_-(B))}.$$ For the plus edge set, $$F_+(B\wedge B')=F_+(B)\cap F_+(B'),\qquad
 F_+(B\vee B')\supseteq F_+(B)\cup F_+(B').$$ Monotonicity and supermodularity of $n$ give its contribution to $w(B\wedge B')w(B\vee B')\geq w(B)w(B')$. For the minus edge set the same argument has meet and join interchanged. Multiplication proves the lattice condition. An indicator of fixed coordinate values preserves this condition, including when one of the compared weights is zero. The standard FKG conditional comparison gives the stated monotonicity.

Alternatively describing $W$ by its vertices, coherence forces equality exactly across $B$-changing edges, in addition to the equalities in $E_0$. There are no other restrictions. Uniform counting gives one independent fair sign per resulting component. ◻

</details>

We also need association and boundary comparison for heights. Coordinatewise minimum and maximum of two integer Lipschitz functions are integer Lipschitz. If their boundary values are ordered, sorting by minimum and maximum preserves the respective boundary conditions. This is the finite-lattice setting of Fortuin–Kasteleyn–Ginibre (Fortuin, Kasteleyn, and Ginibre 1971) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[FKG1971](#ref-FKG1971)]. A fixed boundary or anchor bounds every height by graph distance, so the height coordinates lie in finite chains.

The following finite-state argument proves height association and monotonicity in the boundary values, as well as the spin comparison consequences of <a href="#lem:spin-fkg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spin-fkg">3</a>. Update one unfixed coordinate at a time by its conditional quantile, using independent uniforms and a deterministic cyclic schedule. For heights its conditional support is the integer interval $$[\max_{w\sim v}H(w)-1,\ \min_{w\sim v}H(w)+1],$$ intersected with the fixed coordinate bounds; both endpoints are increasing in the neighboring heights. For the first spin the lattice condition makes its conditional plus probability increasing in all other unfixed spins. Thus each update is increasing in the current configuration and in its uniform random variable. Starting from the least feasible configuration, each finite sequence of updates is an increasing function of independent variables and hence is positively associated, by the one-dimensional Chebyshev inequality and induction on the independent coordinates. The same uniforms couple ordered boundary prescriptions in their indicated order.

These finite chains converge to their counting laws. The spin marginal is positive on every assignment of the unpinned first-color spins, because a constant second color is always coherent. For heights, any configuration can be decreased to the coordinatewise minimum with a second one: lower a discrepant vertex of largest current height by one. A higher neighbor already at its target would contradict the target’s Lipschitz condition, so this move is feasible. Repeating and reversing the corresponding moves for the second configuration proves irreducibility. Each single-coordinate conditional law has a positive holding probability. A cyclic sweep therefore gives an irreducible aperiodic chain preserving the desired law. Passing to its stationary limit proves association and the ordered couplings, including the specified first-color pinnings and second-color equalities.

<a id="stmt-auto-definition-1"></a>
<div class="definition">

**Definition 4** (Double paths). A dual edge is *double-$B$ of sign $t$* if the two primal endpoints of the crossed edge both have $B=t$. A double path or circuit uses only such edges, with one fixed sign throughout. The analogous terminology applies with the colors interchanged.

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-double-cut"></a>
<div id="lem:double-cut" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Exact cut law). *Let $C$ be a simple double-$B$ circuit of sign $t$. Suppose that no edge of $E_0$ crosses $C$. Conditional on the exterior pair configuration and on the $B=t$ values on the inner boundary layer of $C$, the interior law is the uniform coherent-pair law with that $B$ boundary layer, free $W$ boundary values, and just the constraints situated inside $C$.*

*This assertion also holds for a circuit selected by a stopping exploration which reveals only exterior $B$ spins and the $B=t$ cut sites. No $W$ spins need be revealed during that exploration. Along the inner boundary face chain, a height lift takes values in one pair of consecutive integers.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-double-cut"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-double-cut</summary>

*Proof.* Every interaction crossing $C$ is on an edge with $B$ equal at both endpoints. Coherence places no restriction on $W$ across such an edge, and the hypothesis excludes an $E_0$ equality there. Once the cut $B$ values are fixed, the counting weight therefore factors into an exterior factor and the stated interior factor. Fixed constraints inside and outside occur in their respective factors.

For the stopping version, explore from the designated exterior side through primal edges not blocked by a good dual edge, querying the two endpoint $B$ values when needed. If a surrounding double circuit exists in the search collar, this exploration cannot pass it. The separating boundary contains a simple double circuit. The queried sites on its inner side are only its good cut sites; bounded pockets may be filled on the already explored side. Thus, conditional on the exploration record, the same factorization applies. This is a finite exploration statement, so it also applies after arbitrary exterior conditioning of the allowed type.

At a dual vertex of a double path, its two used edges force all three adjacent $B$ spins to equal $t$. The inner face sites of a double circuit therefore form a connected constant-$B$ chain. The two residues with this $B$ value are consecutive. An integer Lipschitz lift along a connected constant-$B$ chain stays in one translated copy of that pair, proving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

We shall impose $W=+1$ along a bounded number of connected paths. It is useful to separate their equality constraints from their labels. First put their edges in $E_0$. Conditional on $B$, each path then has one component label. If there are $m$ paths, the conditional probability of all labels being $+1$ lies between $2^{-m}$ and $1$. If there is just one connected path, this probability is exactly $1/2$, independently of $B$; requiring its plus label does not change the $B$ marginal. We will always account for these labels after an argument involving the $B$ marginal.

<a id="sec:cuts"></a>
### Buffered circuits, mixing, and rare pins

We first obtain double-$B$ circuits from an occupied-site annulus estimate. These circuits give conditional comparisons in fixed geometric buffers and loop-count estimates inside arbitrary simple contours. We then construct the plane law and prove multiplicative comparisons for rare pins. Each stopping cut leaves its strict interior unexamined.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### An annulus input and local circuits

Let $\mathbb H=\mathbb T^*$ be the honeycomb lattice. Its hexagonal faces are identified with the height vertices of $\mathbb T$; face distance means graph distance in $\mathbb T$. In lattice units write $$e_1=(1,0),\qquad e_2=(1/2,\sqrt3/2),\qquad
 |s e_1+t e_2|_g=\max\{|s|,|t|,|s+t|\}.$$ On face centers this norm is triangular graph distance. For a center face $z$, let $\Gamma_n(z)$ be the outer honeycomb contour of the union of closed faces at distance at most $n$ from $z$, and let $\Lambda_n(z)$ be the honeycomb domain strictly inside that contour, excluding its vertices and edges. We suppress $z$ when it is the origin, and round nonintegral indices down. The union of these closed faces, its contour, and the real norm ball of radius $n$ differ in radius by a bounded additive constant.

The *graph inradius* about $z$ of a simple contour $L$ surrounding $z$ is the largest integer $n$ for which $\Lambda_n(z)$ and $\Gamma_n(z)$ lie strictly inside $L$. It differs by a universal additive constant from the minimum graph distance from $z$ to a face incident to $L$. Indeed the smaller filled face balls cannot cross $L$, while the next incident face layer reaches it. In particular an exterior face incident to $L$ can be chosen with center $u$ satisfying $|u-z|_g=n+O(1)$.

Choose one bipartite class $Y$ of honeycomb vertices. Its nearest-neighbor graph is triangular: two $Y$ sites are adjacent when joined by two honeycomb edges. At edge weight one, a site is *occupied plus* if all three incident faces have $B=+1$. A circuit of occupied sites is a circuit in this triangular site graph.

We use the annulus circuit estimate of (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 2.3, p. 9; proof in Appendix B) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[OpenAIWeighted2026](#ref-OpenAIWeighted2026)], specialized to edge weight one: in a regular face-ball domain of radius $2r$, with $B=-1$ on its boundary face layer and $W$ free, an occupied-plus $Y$ circuit surrounding the radius-$r$ ball has probability at least a constant $p>0$, uniformly for all sufficiently large $r$. Boundary-cell factors are excluded in that theorem. At edge weight one their removal leaves exactly the uniform coherent-pair law with the indicated one-spin boundary condition. Only this annulus theorem is used from (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[OpenAIWeighted2026](#ref-OpenAIWeighted2026)].

Fix $0<\varepsilon<1/50$ for the local circuit constructions below.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-circuit"></a>
<div id="lem:local-circuit" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-circuit-annulus"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Local double circuits). *There are $R>3$, $p>0$, and $r_0<\infty$ with the following property. In a constrained coherent-pair law, suppose that the radius-$Rr$ face-ball patch about $z$, including the two layers of its bounding contour, contains no prescribed spins or $E_0$ edges. For $r\ge r_0$, conditional on arbitrary $B$ values outside that patch, there is probability at least $p$ of a simple double-$B$ circuit of either specified sign whose curve surrounds $z$ and lies at norm radii $$\label{eq:local-circuit-annulus}
                (1-\varepsilon)r\le |\,\cdot-z|_g\le(2+\varepsilon)r.$$ The circuit encloses the real norm ball of radius $(1-\varepsilon)r$. For a sign-$t$ circuit, additional $B=t$ pins in the patch are allowed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-circuit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-circuit</summary>

*Proof.* Consider sign plus first. Fix $B=-1$ on both face layers of the radius-$2r$ regular contour. By <a href="#lem:spin-fkg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spin-fkg">3</a> this lowers every increasing event. The exact cut law identifies its interior with the one-spin minus-boundary law in the cited annulus theorem. Bounded changes in the chosen contour and integer radius are absorbed by the fixed slack in <a href="#eq:local-circuit-annulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-circuit-annulus">[eq:local-circuit-annulus]</a>.

Each edge of the occupied $Y$ circuit lifts to two honeycomb edges. Both are double-$B$ plus: the two occupied endpoints prescribe all incident face spins. The resulting closed walk stays a bounded distance from the site circuit and has the same winding about the inner ball. Split the walk at repeated vertices and discard zero-winding pieces; one obtains a simple double circuit with that winding. The $Y$ lattice and the face-center lattice are translates with the same graph norm, so all the displacement errors just used are bounded independently of $r$. For sufficiently large $r$ the resulting circuit satisfies <a href="#eq:local-circuit-annulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-circuit-annulus">[eq:local-circuit-annulus]</a> and has the asserted enclosure.

Choose, for example, $R=4$, increasing the lower threshold on $r$ to accommodate all face stars and contour layers. The comparison takes place inside the prescribed empty patch, so arbitrary exterior $B$ conditioning leaves it valid. Global sign reversal proves the minus case. Adding pins of the requested sign can only increase the circuit probability by <a href="#lem:spin-fkg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spin-fkg">3</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-worst-cut"></a>
<div id="lem:worst-cut" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (A circuit under the opposite cut condition). *There are fixed $K<\infty$ and $c>0$ with the following property. Inside a regular dual cut of radius $Kn$, fix $B=-t$ on the inner boundary layer and leave $W$ free. With probability at least $c$, there is a double-$B$ circuit of sign $t$ between radii $n$ and $2n$. The constants are independent of $n$, for all sufficiently large $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-worst-cut"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-worst-cut</summary>

*Proof.* Take a polygonal curve at norm radius $3n/2$. Choose $r$ to be a sufficiently small fixed multiple of $n$, and cover this curve cyclically by annuli as in <a href="#eq:local-circuit-annulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-circuit-annulus">[eq:local-circuit-annulus]</a>. Put their centers on the curve, including its corners, with successive distances between $1.4r$ and $1.6r$. Integer rounding preserves these inequalities with slack for large $n$. Choose $r/n$ small enough that the annuli and their radius-$Rr$ comparison patches lie strictly between radii $n$ and $2n$.

The circuits in neighboring annuli must intersect. Their enclosed inner balls overlap, and each inner ball protrudes outside the other’s outer ball, since $\varepsilon<1/50$. Disjoint Jordan curves have disjoint or nested interiors, and these two geometric properties exclude both possibilities. At a common honeycomb vertex, two double paths of the same sign join with all three incident face spins of that sign. Choose intersection points for consecutive pairs and join them along the intervening circuits. The resulting closed walk stays $O(r)$ from the center polygon and is homotopic to it in the complement of the radius-$n$ ball. Extracting a simple circuit with nonzero winding gives the required circuit between radii $n$ and $2n$.

There are only a fixed number of small annuli. Their events are all increasing in the order toward $t$, so [lem:spin-fkg,lem:local-circuit] [lem:spin-fkg](#lem:spin-fkg), [lem:local-circuit](#lem:local-circuit) give a positive lower bound for their intersection. A fixed sufficiently large $K$ keeps every comparison patch away from the opposite boundary condition. The constants do not depend on $n$. ◻

</details>

For clarity, a *buffer* below means an open neighborhood in which the local annuli, together with their larger worst-condition cuts, fit with positive clearance. Thus an obstacle can lie in the hole of a collar without lying in the buffers used to construct the collar circuit. We distinguish these local buffers from the entire disk bounded by the eventual circuit.

<a id="stmt-lem-buffer-circuits"></a>
<div id="lem:buffer-circuits" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Circuits in buffers). *Consider a fixed positive-width polygonal collar, or a fixed positive-width tube following a polygonal arc. Suppose it has a buffer disjoint from $E_0$ and from all prescribed $B$ spins. At sufficiently fine mesh, conditional on arbitrary $B$ values away from the buffer, there is a probability at least $c>0$ of a double-$B$ circuit around the collar’s hole, or a connected double-$B$ path following the tube, of either specified sign. For a sign-$t$ event, arbitrary additional $B=t$ pins in the buffer are allowed. The constant depends on the fixed geometry, not on the mesh, the number of pins elsewhere, or their values.*

<a id="eq:stack-failure"></a>
*Finitely many separated such requirements have a uniformly positive joint probability, even if different buffers request different signs. For a logarithmic stack of separated similar annular buffers between radii $r$ and $L$, the probability that no circuit of a prescribed sign occurs is at most $$\label{eq:stack-failure}
 C(r/L)^\alpha$$ for some $\alpha>0$. In lattice units this holds uniformly for $L\gg r\geq1$, after adjustment of $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-buffer-circuits"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-buffer-circuits</summary>

*Proof.* First consider a small regular annulus whose larger cut lies in the buffer. Delete any favorable pins in that cut. This can only lower the probability of the desired event in the order toward $t$. Fix the opposite sign on both sides of the larger deterministic cut. FKG lowers the desired probability again, and the exact cut law identifies the remaining problem with <a href="#lem:worst-cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:worst-cut">7</a>. This gives a uniform local lower bound under every allowed exterior conditioning.

We explain the geometric gluing, with all comparison patches inside the buffer. Cover the center curve of the tube or collar by finitely many small regular annuli. Choose their common inner radius much smaller than the tube width. Consecutive centers may be separated by, for example, $3/2$ times that radius in the graph-distance norm. Their inner balls intersect, and each inner ball protrudes beyond the other’s outer ball. Two surrounding circuits in these annuli must therefore intersect: disjoint Jordan curves would have disjoint interiors or nested interiors, and the two geometric conditions exclude these possibilities. Their union is connected and follows the prescribed tube. Around a closed center curve, its outer boundary contains a simple circuit enclosing the hole; the annuli are small enough that their union does not fill that hole. At a common dual vertex, double paths of the same sign have all three adjacent spin sites of that sign, so the gluing does not lose the double-path property. Bounded lattice rounding is absorbed by the positive geometric clearances.

All the small annulus events for one sign are increasing in the same order. FKG multiplies their local lower bounds, giving a positive constant depending only on the finite cover. For separated buffers, condition successively on the configurations outside the next buffer; the preceding lower bound applies under each such conditioning. This also permits different signs in different buffers.

Finally choose a fixed scale ratio large enough that the enlarged buffers of successive annular slots are disjoint. There are at least $c_2\log(L/r)-C_2$ slots. At each trial, conditional on the previously examined slots, success has probability at least $c_3>0$. Failure in all slots has probability at most $(1-c_3)^{c_2\log(L/r)-C_2}$, which is <a href="#eq:stack-failure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stack-failure">[eq:stack-failure]</a>. The finitely many bounded lattice scales can be absorbed in its prefactor. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Loop estimates inside simple contours

For a simple honeycomb contour $L$, let $\mathbb P_L$ be the loop $O(2)$ law strictly inside $L$, with edge weight one and empty exterior: loops use neither edges nor vertices of $L$, and have weight $2^{\#\mathrm{loops}}$. It is represented by coherent pairs on the interior faces with both spins plus on the inner boundary layer. Indeed no wall can meet a contour vertex, since all interior faces incident to that vertex are boundary faces. Conversely a loop strictly inside $L$ encloses no boundary face. Given the loops, assigning independently to each loop the color $B$ or $W$ that changes across it reconstructs a unique coherent pair with these boundary signs. Thus the colors are independent fair choices conditional on the loop geometry.

The first-spin marginal under this flat condition still admits the comparisons above. Embed $L$ in a larger regular spin domain, fix $B=+$ on the exterior faces and the inner boundary layer, and join all those sites by $W$-equality edges. The exterior and boundary face chain are connected, so one connected equality set suffices. Requiring its single $W$ label to be plus has probability exactly $1/2$ conditional on every $B$, by <a href="#lem:spin-fkg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spin-fkg">3</a>; it does not change the $B$ marginal. The resulting inside pair law is precisely the flat law just described.

<a id="stmt-prop-loop-estimates"></a>
<div id="prop:loop-estimates" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9** (Loop estimates). *There are constants $c_0>0$ and $0<\rho<1$ such that, for all sufficiently large integer $n$, the following statements hold.*

1.  *If the interior of a simple contour $L$ contains $\Lambda_{2n}(z)$, then $\mathbb P_L$ gives probability at least $c_0$ to a loop contained in $\Lambda_{2n}(z)$ and surrounding $\Lambda_n(z)$.*

<a id="eq:upper-loop-estimate"></a>
2.  *If $L$ has graph inradius $n$ about $z$, then $$\label{eq:upper-loop-estimate}
     \mathbb P_L\bigl(\text{at least two loops surround }\Lambda_{\rho n}(z)\bigr)
     \le 1-c_0.$$*

*There is no aspect-ratio or boundary-regularity assumption on $L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-loop-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-loop-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* For (i), use two separated polygonal collars at norm radii, for example, $1.3n$ and $1.7n$. Their comparison buffers fit strictly between radii $n$ and $2n$ and avoid the fixed boundary layers and equality edges. [lem:buffer-circuits](#lem:buffer-circuits) gives a uniformly positive probability of a double-$B$ plus circuit in the inner collar and a double-$B$ minus circuit in the outer collar. Different signs cause no association issue: reveal the outer collar and apply the local conditional bound in the disjoint inner buffer. A $B$ wall separates these opposite-sign circuits. No wall can cross either double circuit, since all three face spins at each of its vertices agree. At least one wall loop therefore surrounds the inner circuit and lies inside the outer one, proving (i).

For (ii), put $z=0$. If a honeycomb circuit surrounds $\Lambda_s$, its inner incident faces contain a triangular face-center circuit surrounding the real norm ball of radius $s-b$, for a universal $b$. To see this, follow the inner incident faces along the contour. Successive centers are equal or adjacent, and the resulting closed walk tracks the contour within bounded distance. Its edges lie on the inner side. Splitting at repeated vertices gives a simple circuit with the same nonzero winding about the smaller ball. The same observation applies to a double circuit whose face spins have one fixed sign.

<a id="eq:two-loop-coloring"></a>
Let $E$ be the event in <a href="#eq:upper-loop-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:upper-loop-estimate">[eq:upper-loop-estimate]</a>, and let $E'$ also require that its outermost surrounding loop changes $W$ and its second outermost changes $B$. The independent coloring gives $$\label{eq:two-loop-coloring}
                         \mathbb P_L(E')=\tfrac14\mathbb P_L(E).$$ On $E'$, the outer loop is double-$B$ plus: no surrounding $B$ wall lies outside it. The faces immediately inside the next loop have $B=-1$. Consequently there is a simple minus face-center circuit enclosing the real norm ball of radius $\rho n-b$. Other loops cannot alter these incident signs without intersecting one of the two selected loops.

We select an enclosing plus cut using only $B$. Extend the flat spins as plus outside $L$, and start with all exterior faces reached. Traverse face adjacency unless the crossed honeycomb edge, including its endpoints, is strictly inside $L$ and is double-$B$ plus. A deterministic queue queries the $B$ values needed to decide each attempted traversal, and retains the full query record. Complete this reachable closure. If the center is unreached, let $U$ be its component in the ordinary face-adjacency graph of all unreached faces. Each face adjacent to $U$ but outside it is reached, and each edge separating the two sets is an eligible strictly interior double-plus edge. The reached set is connected to the exterior, so $U$ has no holes.
